# European Economic Perspectives Europe's demographic challenge

## Europe's demographic transition is gaining pace, with major consequences

Europe is undergoing a dramatic demographic shift: Between now and 2050, according to the UN, the EU population is expected to shrink from 447m to 423m − a decline of 5.4%. The fall in the working-age population, which has been underway for a decade already, is likely to be even more pronounced − from 286m to 237m, a reduction of 17%. This means that, each year, the EU working-age population is going to decline on average by c1.7m, or 0.6% of the total working-age population. The demographic transition does not just imply that Europe will have fewer and fewer people working; it also means that Europe's average (working) age is increasing. All combined, the demographic transition will have major consequences for European GDP trend growth, productivity and spending patterns, as well as public finances; there might also be an impact on inflation, neutral interest rates and asset prices. In this report, we will first describe the key features of the European demographic transition and show which countries face the greatest challenges. We will then explain why immigration and measures to raise labour force participation can only partially mitigate the demographic forces, but not compensate them. Last but not least, we will lay out the economic consequences of ageing in Europe.

## Southern Europe is facing the most unfavourable demographic trend

Based on six key demographic indicators, we draw the following conclusions: First, across the larger European countries, southern ones − Italy, Portugal and Greece − are currently experiencing the most unfavourable demographic trends, while Nordic nations − Sweden, Norway and Denmark − appear to be relatively better positioned. Second, in Italy, Portugal and Greece, but also Spain, demographic trends look set to get even worse over the coming decades. Between now and 2050, the Spanish working-age population will likely decline by 290k per year, or 0.9% p.a., while the working-age population in Italy is expected to fall by 370k p.a., i.e. 0.7%. Outside of the Eurozone, Poland faces the most unfavourable demographic trend over the next two decades. Third, across the large Eurozone countries outside of the south of Europe, Germany is in the least favourable position (with the working-age population set to shrink by 8m, or 0.6% p.a., by 2050), while France is relatively better positioned (with the working-age population expected to shrink by 2.6m, or 0.2% p.a., by 2050).

## Migration and higher labour participation are not enough to offset ageing

Two forces could mitigate the negative demographic trend: higher labour participation and immigration. However, within realistic ranges and numbers, none of the two appears to be able to fully compensate the decline in the working-age population. The positive impact from immigration would also be moderated somewhat by the challenges of integrating immigrants into the labour force and by skill mismatches. As regards the latter, less than 30% of the foreign-born population in the EU have a higher level of education, implying that immigration on its own is unlikely to solve the skill shortages in higher-skilled sectors.

## Economic impact and policy implications

The total cost of ageing (i.e. spending on pensions, healthcare, long-term care, etc) was estimated at 24.2% of EU GDP in 2023 and is expected to rise to 25.5% by 2050, according to the European Commission. Ageing will affect many aspects of the economy and society; we concentrate on trend growth, productivity, fiscal sustainability, inflation and wages, neutral interest rates and asset prices. Crucially, the impact on each of these areas is not necessarily one-sided. According to the IMF, policies to mitigate the effect of ageing should focus on closing gender gaps and extending working lives.

## Europe's demographic challenge

In this part of the report, we describe the long-term demographic trends across Europe and compare them with other major economies (the US, Canada, China, Japan and Australia). We use the 2022 Revision of World Population Prospects, which provides population estimates and projections between 1950 and 2099.

Europe is undergoing a demographic shift, as a result of which the EU population is likely to shrink from currently 447m to 423m by 2050, in other words, a contraction of c24m or 5.4% (c16m or 4.8% for the Eurozone).  Declining fertility rates and a rise in life expectancy over the past 50 years, in turn, imply not only a decreasing but also an ageing population in Europe. The latter has a broad set of socioeconomic implications, varying from the working-age population, which at the EU level will likely be shrinking by c1.8m people per year over the coming years, resulting in a total loss of almost 50m people by 2050, structural shifts in the labour market in terms of employment of specific demographic groups, and changes in relative demand for, e.g., healthcare and long term care jobs, as well as broader implications for the countries' fiscal positions.

Below, we look at a subset of six key demographic indicators by country, which we then use to create two ranking tables of the 29 European countries in our sample (comprising the EU, the UK and Switzerland): (1) favourability of demographic trends in the current decade (2020-29); and (2) countries' prospects by 2050. We draw three key conclusions: First, across the larger European countries, southern ones − Italy, Portugal and Greece − are currently experiencing the most unfavourable demographic trends, while the Nordics (Sweden, Norway and Denmark) appear to be relatively better positioned. Specifically, Italy is looking at an annual loss of working-age population of c390k p.a. over the next decade while, in contrast, the working-age population in Sweden is likely to continue expanding moderately (+3k p.a.) over the same time <sup>(2)</sup> . Second, the demographic trends in southern Europe, with Spain added to the list of countries mentioned above, are likely to remain unfavourable in the coming decades. Between now and 2050, the annual decline in working-age population in Spain is projected to be 290k p.a., the same pace as in Germany. The pace of decline in Italian working-age population is expected to be even faster at 370k p.a. Outside of the Eurozone countries, Poland stands out as the country with the sharpest projected decline in demographic trends over the next two decades. Third, across the large Eurozone countries outside of the south of Europe, Germany is in the least favourable position, while France is relatively better positioned.

## 1. Population growth

Across 29 European countries in our sample, 10 countries are set to see their population declining in the current decade, with this number likely increasing to 19 by 2050. In level terms, the EU population is projected to shrink by 24m, from 447m in 2022 to 423m in 2050 (Figure 2). The equivalent decline in the Eurozone is projected to be 16m to 328m in 2050.

EU population is on track to lose c24m by 2050, while working-age population could shrink by c50m

EU population is projected to shrink by 24m by 2050, while Eurozone is set to lose 16m

Figure 1: Average population growth, 2020-29 (%, p.a.)
![](images/39ca60d45d9084a6680ba08cb10294ab86a6627052eb467307eadba552c57b6f.jpg)
Source: UN World Population Prospects, Haver, UBS

Figure 2: Change in European population by 2050 (million people)
![](images/65eb60f1946ba83223425c6beb67dffd015c6f398f917275744cded877ff971c.jpg)
Source: UN World Population Prospects, Haver, UBS

Across the larger European countries, southern ones − Italy, Greece and Portugal − are currently experiencing the fastest rates of decline in population growth. In contrast, Switzerland, Sweden and Norway are seeing the fastest positive growth rates. The largest European economy − Germany − saw moderately positive population growth at the start of this decade, but is now facing a prolonged trend of declining population. Compared with Germany, population dynamics in France appear relatively more optimistic, with population growth projected to turn negative only in the late 2040s. Outside of the large Eurozone countries, Poland stands out as the country likely experiencing one of the fastest deteriorations in population dynamics between the current decade and 2050.

Compared with other major economies − the US, Canada, China, Japan and Australia − European projected population growth between now and 2050 is closer to that in China and Japan, with the US, Canada and Australia doing relatively better. Over the very long term (by 2100), however, the population in China is likely to shrink faster than that in Europe.

Compared with other major economies, population growth in Europe is slower than in the US, Canada and Australia

## 2. Working-age population

Europe has been facing a decline in working-age population for over a decade now, with the exception of a handful of countries (Figure 6). The average annual loss in workingage population since 2010 has amounted to 762k (0.2% of total population) at the EU level and 316k (0.1% of total population) at the Eurozone level.

Europe has been facing a decline in working-age population for over a decade now

Source: Eurostat, UBS
2023 was an exception due to a spike in Ukrainian refugees

In contrast to the trend over the previous decade, 2023 was a year when most European countries saw a rise in their working-age population. However, given that the increase was driven by the influx of Ukrainian refugees, we view this increase as a one-off and expect the working-age population to return to the declining trend in the coming years.

Figure 7: Ukrainian citizens under temporary protection (thousands of people, as of February 2024)
![](images/47c0d8920151ca4bf7a429485a006c85abe50db5babe0619c7cc86d15492af19.jpg)
Source: Eurostat, UBS
EU (Eurozone) working-age population is projected to be declining by c1.8m (c1.4m) p.a. over the coming years

The ageing of Europe, which is set to accelerate in the coming years amid the upcoming retirement of the baby-boomer generation born during the 1950s and 1960s, implies that the decline in working-age population (15-64 years) is likely to be even faster than the decline in overall population. Based on UN population statistics, we calculate that EU working-age population will be shrinking by c1.8m people per year over the coming years, resulting in a total loss of almost 50m working-age people by 2050 compared with 2022. Similarly, the Eurozone is projected to be losing c1.4m people per year from the working-age cohort, with a total decline of around 38m by 2050 (Figure 9).

Figure 8: Average working-age population growth, 2020- 29 (%, p.a.)
![](images/ead4e5546d40e68e1480fe2157536ec5bcbcfc883f3245aba429ced1b5e5785e.jpg)
Source: UN World Population Prospects, Haver, UBS

Figure 9: Change in European working-age population by 2050 (million people)
![](images/f6fcead81acff694cf8fa2b87c480c5ea7fec0bf4295575ba9c679c5bce3a1ff.jpg)
Source: UN World Population Prospects, Haver, UBS

Across the large Eurozone countries, Italy, Germany and Spain are projected to experience the largest declines in working-age population by 2050, 8-10m each. The average annual decline in working-age population in Spain is projected to be 290k p.a., the same pace as in Germany. The pace of decline in Italian working-age population is expected to be even faster at 370k p.a. Outside of the Eurozone countries, Poland stands out as the country with the sharpest projected decline in demographic trends over the next two decades.

Italy, Germany and Spain are each expected to lose 8-10m in workingage population by 2050

## 3. Median-age/old-age dependency

Higher life expectancy and low fertility rates over the past 50 years imply that the European population is getting older. In fact, with a population-weighted average age of 44.1 years, Europe is the oldest region across the major economies, with the exception of Japan. At the country level, Italy has the highest median age, while Ireland enjoys the lowest. Looking ahead, the median age is set to increase across the region over the coming decades.

With the exception of Japan, Europe is the oldest region across the major economies, with the median age set to increase further over the coming years

An ageing and shrinking working-age population implies a rise in the old-age dependency ratio, i.e. the number of people aged over 65 years per 100 people of working age. Italy stands out in this category with currently the highest old-age dependency ratio, which is also expected to remain the highest in the region over the next two decades. While the old-age dependency ratios are expected to rise across the region, the smallest increase over the coming years is expected in the Nordics (Denmark, Norway and Sweden).

Italy has the highest old-age dependency ratio in Europe and other major economies (except Japan)

## 4. Fertility rates

In developed countries (with low child and infant mortality rates), the replacement fertility level, at which the population replaces itself from one generation to another, is 2.1 children per woman. Fertility rates in Europe have been on a declining trend for over 50 years now, with a particular dip around the 1990s and the global financial crisis (GFC). As a result, fertility rates across all European countries currently stand below the replacement level.

Across the larger European countries, the lowest fertility rates are currently observed in the south of Europe, in Spain and Italy, closely followed by Portugal and Greece. With a population-weighted average fertily rate of 1.5, on aggregate, Europe is doing similarly to other major economies, in line with Canada, and slightly behind Australia and the US.

Looking ahead, based on the UN's "medium scenario", fertility rates are expected to show only marginal improvements by 2050 and are not foreseen to return to the replacement rate of 2.1. However, we acknowledge significant uncertainty around this assumption with a chance of more public spending on family policies potentially boosting fertility rates.

Fertility rates across all European countries stand below the replacement level

Fertility rates in Europe are not foreseen to return to the replacement rate of 2.1

## 5. Life expectancy

Life expectancy in Europe has, on average, increased by over 10 years since the 1960s. Across the larger European countries, Italy and Portugal have seen the largest increases since the 1960s. In an international comparison, Europe is behind Japan, Canada and Australia, but ahead of the US and China. Looking ahead, life expectancy is likely to continue to rise over the coming decades, though with little change in terms of relative country performance.

Life expectancy in Europe has, on average, increased by over 10 years since the 1960s, and is likely to continue rising over the coming decades

## Bringing it all together − which countries are most vulnerable?

Figures 22 and 23 show the relative country ranking, reflecting: (1) the favourability of demographic trends in the current decade (2020-29); and (2) countries' prospects by 2050.

The south of Europe is facing the most unfavourable demographic trend, while the Nordics are relatively better positioned

We draw three key conclusions: First, across the larger European countries, southern ones − Italy, Portugal and Greece − are currently experiencing the most unfavourable demographic trends, while the Nordics (Sweden, Norway and Denmark) appear to be relatively better positioned.

Second, the demographic trends in southern Europe, with Spain added to the list of countries mentioned above, are likely to remain unfavourable in the coming decades. Between now and 2050, the annual decline in working-age population in Spain is projected to be 290k p.a., the same pace as in Germany - despite the much lower population size. The pace of decline in Italian working-age population is expected to be even faster at 370k p.a. Outside of the Eurozone countries, Poland stands out as the country with the sharpest projected decline in demographic trends over the next two decades.

Third, across the large Eurozone countries outside of the south of Europe, Germany is in the least favourable position, while France is relatively better positioned.

## Mitigating factors

## Rising labour force participation

The trend of a declining working-age population has been accompanied by a rise in labour participation rates, implying that, out of a shrinking pool of available workers, more have joined the labour force. But crucially, this rise is not sufficient to fully offset the shrinkage of the working-age population.

The rise in participation rates is not sufficient to offset the shrinkage of working-age population

The increase in participation rates has been largely driven by higher participation of older workers (55-64 years old) as well as female workers (Figures 25-26), which historically have been below the aggregate participation rates. In theory, if Eurozone female and older workers' participation rates were to rise to 75% (the aggregate participation rate), this could add 8.2m workers (11m for the EU), compared to an expected 38m loss in Eurozone working-age population (c50m for the EU) by 2050 . In practice, however, the scope for further increases in activity of female and particularly older workers is likely limited in the absence of further reforms, especially around retirement.

## Immigration

Migration is often mentioned as a potential solution to the shrinking domestic population. While in terms of age structure perspective, the foreign-born population indeed tends to be younger and mostly of working age, there are a number of factors why migration alone cannot be a solution to the deteriorating demographic trends. First, the number of incoming migrants is not enough to offset the loss in working-age population. Second, countries are facing challenges in integrating migrants into the labour force. Third, less than 30% of the foreign-born population in the EU and Eurozone have a higher level of education, implying some of the higher-skilled sectors are likely to get less help from higher migration.

Migration alone cannot be a solution to the deteriorating demographic trends

On balance, the share of foreign population across Europe has been on the rise, with the exception of a few countries. In general, the shares have been the highest in western and northern Europe, while they have been the lowest in central and eastern Europe.

Positively, in the overwhelming majority of European countries, the foreign-born population is younger than the domestic population and skewed towards those of working age (Figures 32-33).

Figure 32: EU domestic and foreign-born population age structure, 2023
![](images/d200ef5389c0de8555988c5ebb7d8ff67958fa7d57d731f92ee4ca314fd92392.jpg)
Source: Eurostat, UBS

Figure 33: Eurozone domestic and foreign-born population age structure, 2023
![](images/d1b40af96c238569f4a48f09cb3e656f80dd5a9da675247e4d45cba9888d0450.jpg)
Source: Eurostat, UBS

Figure 34: Median age of domestic-born and foreign-born population, 2022
![](images/357cfb974942d27e410a7c0de7df237dd3fa0b360aff2624c27609d189f46812.jpg)
Source: Eurostat, UBS

As a result, the share of foreign-born workers in total employment has also been on the rise across most European countries (Figure 35).

However, we see three factors limiting the offset that migration can provide to deteriorating domestic demographics.

First, the number of incoming migrants accounts for only a fraction of the losses in working-age population.

The number of incoming migrants accounts for only a fraction of the losses in working-age population

Figure 36: Average annual changes in working-age population and migration
![](images/dadf1d1a349262c1bfbc33a6527e6a143048f1d01ebb5d3b7889b06777df5f12.jpg)
Source: Eurostat, UBS calculations
\* Annual averages are calculated over the period 2009-19. For the EU, the relevant period is 2014-19.
Countries are still facing challenges in integrating migrants into the labour force

Second, countries face difficulties in integrating migrants into the labour markets. One way to gauge labour market integration is via the gap between the shares of the foreign-born population in total population and in employment. Figure 37 shows that, in the majority of European countries, the share of foreigners in the total population exceeds their share in employment, suggesting only partial integration. Among those foreign workers that have been seeking work, language skills and the lack of qualification recognition remain the main obstacles to finding a suitable job (Figure 39).

Figure 37: Foreign-born population and employment shares (2023)
![](images/ed9e5ebea3fe08181dc97fe8047dbfeee5d735415be3469853af8c0ea9b04c34.jpg)
Source: Eurostat, UBS calculations

Figure 38: Change in skill level from last job (2021)
![](images/4fb4c6147657817d2333cdad0854b41fa24cbd454e1d01f6cd8af843ddc9f8f2.jpg)
Source: Eurostat, UBS

Figure 39: Obstacles to finding a suitable job\*
![](images/2df3626fd3433ef33b4eff6b1df8429fe2bc84b62deefad2eda791272f8fac8e.jpg)
Source: Eurostat, UBS. \* Perceived obstacles as reported by participants in a 2021 labour force survey.
Education background of foreign workers is skewed towards lower levels

Third, the education background of foreign workers in most European countries is skewed towards lower levels. This implies that the majority of workers are likely to go into lower-skilled jobs, with relatively fewer potential workers available for some of the higher-skilled sectors (Figure 40).

Figure 40: Foreign population, level of education (2023)

## Impact on the economy and policy implications

In this section, we focus on the potential economic and policy implications due to an ageing population. In addition to perhaps the most discussed area of trend growth, Bodnár and Nerlich (2022) outline five areas that are likely to be affected by the ageing population: productivity, neutral policy rates, inflation and wages, asset and house prices, and fiscal sustainability (Figure 41). Crucially, the impact on each of those areas is not one-sided, with some offsetting factors and caveats around the stage of the demographic transition.

There are six areas that are likely to be affected by ageing: trend growth, productivity, neutral policy rates, inflation and wages, asset and house prices, and fiscal sustainability

Figure 41: Potential areas of impact of ageing population
![](images/913e16921c0c46ef6c72bc2fed76a1dca1d9aec225a92de434a34d7310e505d6.jpg)
Source: ECB Occasional Paper Series, The macroeconomic and fiscal impact of population ageing

## Trend growth

Trend growth can be defined as a function of three factors: labour, capital and total factor productivity. All else equal, the decline in labour input would imply lower trend growth. However, changes in capital (investment) and productivity, which we discuss in more detail below, might accelerate or mitigate the negative impact on trend growth. As regards investment, on the one hand, slower population growth and lower labour supply might encourage companies to invest more into automation (and AI). In this scenario, the increase in capital could offset the decline in labour. On the other hand, if companies expect the declining population to lead to lower demand, it might discourage companies' investments. In this scenario, the decline in capital input could be an additional drag on long-term growth. According to the IMF's long-term growth projections, with the exception of a handful of countries, trend growth across Europe is

Trend growth in most European countries is likely to decline by the end of this decade compared with the pre-pandemic average

likely to decline by the end of this decade compared with the pre-pandemic average (Figure 42).

## Productivity

While older workers bring a higher level of experience, it could be argued that, over time, this positive impact may be offset by a moderation of their physical and cognitive abilities. Such a hump-shaped relationship between age and productivity would imply that the increasing cohort of older population could be a drag on productivity. The empirical evidence on this issue, however, is mixed. <sup>(4)</sup> Furthermore, there are a number of potentially offsetting factors.

Over time, experience of older workers may be offset by a moderation of physical and cognitive abilities negatively affecting productivity

First, a rising educational level implies likely more productive future older workers. Second, increasing longevity and health imply that the point at which abilities start to decline is likely to occur later in life. Third, declining productivity of individuals might not lead to the same decline in productivity at a firm level as, e.g. special equipment may be provided to older workers. Fourth, structural policies (e.g. training) might be implemented. Fifth, there could potentially be productivity-enhancing innovations.

## Fiscal sustainability

Ageing implies higher public spending on pensions, healthcare and long-term care. According to the European Commission's 2024 Ageing Report, the total cost of ageing, which is defined as spending on pensions, healthcare, long-term care and education, stood at 24.8% of Eurozone GDP in 2023 (24.2% at the EU level), similar to Japan, but this compares with below c15% in the US. The cost is expected to rise by another 0.5pp by the end of this decade and 1.6pp by 2050. Spending on pensions accounts for the largest share of total spending on ageing − 12% of GDP in the Eurozone (11.4% in the EU) or just under 50% of total spending on ageing. Rising spending on pensions is also the key driver of the projected increase in the total cost of ageing, accounting for all of the increase by 2030, and around 50% of the increase by 2050.

Total cost of ageing stood at 24.8% of Eurozone GDP in 2023 (24.2% at the EU level) and is expected to rise by another 0.5pp by the end of the decade

Figure 44: Total cost of ageing\* (% of GDP)
![](images/c4da768fe0e90136139f5a7786fd2af462154f588277d68342f1ed517aa97795.jpg)
Source: European Commission, 2024 Ageing Report
\* Spending on pensions, healthcare, long-term care and education.

Figure 45: Spending on pensions (% of GDP)
![](images/633ce9f63c2e539f17c9681e3dd3bca10b5076823f7b97d32dd3076d29366d51.jpg)
Source: European Commission, 2024 Ageing Report

Aside from the direct increase in costs, ageing could have a negative fiscal impact through a decline in tax revenues. First, the declining labour force could lead to lower revenues from income tax and social security contributions. Second, other tax revenues (e.g. VAT) could also be negatively affected by a shift in consumption patterns, with higher consumption of (often price-regulated) services and less consumption of durable goods. On the flipside, however, a potential increase in capital gain tax revenues due to higher demand for precautionary savings amid higher life expectancy could provide some offset to otherwise likely falling tax revenue.

Ageing could have a negative fiscal impact through a decline in tax revenues

4. Aiyar, S., Ebeke, C. and Shao, X. (2016), The Impact of Workforce Aging on European Productivity, IMF Working Papers, No WP/16/238, December 2016. Poplawski-Ribeiro, M. (2020), Labour force ageing and productivity growth, Applied Economic Letters, Vol. 27, No 6, IMF, 16 July 2019, pp. 498-502. Stoeldraijer, L. and Ours, J. V. (2010), Age, wage and productivity, CEPR Discussion Papers, No 7713, Centre for Economic Policy Research, London. Börsch-Supan, A. and Weiss, M. (2016), Productivity and age: Evidence from work teams at the assembly line, The Journal of the Economics of Ageing, Vol.7, April, pp. 30-42.

In terms of long-term fiscal sustainability, particularly among high-debt countries, rising costs and declining tax revenues due to ageing imply risks of cuts to non-ageing-related public spending, which could negatively affect potential growth. What does this mean for policymakers and what can be done to ease the negative impact of ageing?

According to Amaglobeli et al. (2019), <sup>(5)</sup> policies to mitigate the effect of ageing should focus on reforms aimed at closing gender gaps and extending working lives amid a rising life expectancy. Such policies could include measures ensuring equality of remuneration, provision of childcare services, and adjustment in taxes and benefits, making early retirement less attractive. In addition, adopting education and training to better align with technological change, and resulting compositional changes in labour demand, could also help to mitigate the impact of ageing to some extent.

## Neutral interest rate

Demographic transition is likely to have an impact on the neutral interest rate through several channels working in different directions. There are factors that are likely to lower the neutral rate: (1) an increase in the capital-labour ratio as a result of shrinking labour supply and the corresponding decline in the marginal product of capital − a lower marginal product of capital makes borrowing less attractive, hence exerting downward pressure on interest rates (see also IMF, 2023); (2) a lower share of younger workers potentially slowing innovation and productivity growth; (3) higher risk aversion and demand for safe assets by the older population; and (4) higher precautionary savings amid a rising life expectancy.

Demographic transition is likely to have an impact on the neutral interest rate through several channels working in different directions

On the flipside, given the rising share of the older population, typically dissavers, and the declining share of working-age population, typically accumulating savings, total savings could decline, implying a positive impact on the neutral rate.

## Inflation and wages

Using Japan as a benchmark, the ageing of the domestic population has often been considered as a disinflationary factor. However, similar to the impact on the neutral interest rate, ageing implies both inflationary and disinflationary changes, with the net impact being uncertain.

Ageing implies both inflationary and disinflationary changes, with the net impact being uncertain

In general, a high share of (very) young and old people who consume but do not participate in production, and hence do not add to labour supply, would imply an inflationary impact. With ageing in Europe over the last decade accompanied by increasing participation rates, particularly among older workers, supporting labour supply, the overall impact so far has been disinflationary. However, with the European baby boomers (born in the 1950s and 1960s) retiring, implying a decline in labour supply, the impact of ageing could turn inflationary.

Among other things, ageing is likely to lead to changes in relative consumption amid higher demand for services by older people. In addition, consumption by older people tends to be skewed towards price-regulated goods (e.g. medicine) and services (e.g. healthcare) and is, hence, less demand-sensitive. This could imply some dampening effect, although likely very small, on inflation.

Similarly, the impact on wages is not straightforward. On the one hand, wage levels tend to increase with age, while wage growth tends to slow down. On the other hand, with baby boomers retiring and labour supply of older workers declining, premiums for their wages might rise. More broadly, declining labour supply accompanied by labour shortages could also lead to stronger bargaining power and, hence, higher wage growth for workers.

## Asset and house prices

In general, working-age populations are viewed as savers and the cohort that accumulates assets in preparation for retirement, while those already retired are viewed as dissavers. With the retirement of the baby boomers, the number of retirees will increase substantially, which could thus exert some downward pressure on asset prices. In addition, as older people are likely to be more risk-averse in terms of their investment preferences, they might skew their investment portfolios towards fixed-income products rather than equities. However, given the slow-moving nature of ageing, the impact on asset prices is unlikely to be sudden.

The increasing number of retirees, who tend to be dissavers, could exert some downward pressure on asset prices

In a similar vein, the impact of ageing on house prices is not straightforward. On the one hand, demand for credit tends to decline at the end of the working career. This is particularly the case for mortgages, but also for consumer credit, as consumption of older people tends to move away from durable goods towards services. On the other hand, a combination of higher life expectancy, higher living standards in terms of space per person, and the older population's preference for living on their own rather than in multigenerational households, is likely to be supportive for housing demand.

## Valuation Method and Risk Statement

Risks include macroeconomic variables (such as GDP growth rates and inflation), economic slowdown, a weakening currency, global economic events, and government policy changes.
